[^294]: The Ruthenian Greek Catholic hierarchy commemoration formula characteristic of the 1906 Lviv Stauropegion edition: "Let us pray for our most holy universal Pontiff [Name], Pope of Rome" (*Помолимся о святѣйшомъ вселенскомъ Архиереи Папѣ Римскомъ*), reflecting Union with Rome.
[^295]: The ecclesiarch's daylight rule: Great Compline in Great Lent must be concluded while natural daylight remains (*знаменїю дне, сирѣчь прежде мрака*), ensuring monastics return to their cells in quiet contemplation before total darkness.
[^296]: Patristic apologia for the full daily cycle of psalmody and prostrations: a solemn admonition that no monk or abbot, however authoritative, has the right to neglect or diminish the liturgical order instituted by the holy Fathers.
[^297]: Daily monastic measure of 300 prostrations to the ground in church throughout Great Lent (excluding Midnight Office), and the Palestinian monastic rule for cell recitation of the complete 150-psalm Psalter either daily, thrice weekly, or twice weekly.
[^298]: Clean Tuesday Matins (*Во вторникъ первыя недѣли святаго поста на утрени*): Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) following the second poetic stichology of the Psalter, Tone 2, praising the grace of the Fast exemplified by Moses and the Three Holy Youths, and urging the soul to repentance like the Publican.
[^299]: Clean Tuesday Matins: Sessional Hymn (Kathisma) following the third poetic stichology of the Psalter, Tone 2, Model: *O Lord, the cup of torment* (*Господи, чашу муки*), praising the keeping of the second day of the Fast (*двоеденствующе*), asking for peaceful hearts and a well-traversed course of fasting.
[^300]: Matins Canon, Ode 2, Tone 2, Heirmos: "See, see that I am He Who saved in the sea" (Deut. 32:39), appointed with the second biblical canticle of Moses, urging the soul to sobriety, tears, and spiritual ascent unto the mountain of virtues.
[^301]: Matins Canon, Ode 8, Tone 2, Heirmos: "In the bush to Moses" / "Creator of creation", recalling the Three Holy Youths and Daniel delivered through fasting, and urging the soul to cast off sloth and go forth to meet the torch-bearing Bridegroom.
[^302]: Matins Canon, Ode 9, Tone 2, Heirmos: "Who from among the earthborn has heard" / "Rejoice, O Isaiah", calling the faithful to put on the armor of light (Rom. 13:12) and overcome the adversary through fasting, even as the Lord vanquished the tempter in the wilderness.
[^303]: Matins Aposticha self-governing sticheron (*самогласенъ*), Tone 3: "Let us begin, O peoples, the blameless fast", anointing our heads with the oil of almsgiving and washing our faces with the water of purity (Matt. 6:16–18), crying aloud the Lord's Prayer without vain babbling.
[^304]: Sixth Hour of Clean Tuesday: Troparion of prophecy, Tone 1 (*As sojourners are we upon the earth*, 1 Chron. 29:15), Prokeimenon from Psalm 5 (*Give ear unto the voice of my prayer*), and the reading from the Prophet Isaiah (Isa. 1:19–2:3) warning of the sword and fire upon Jerusalem's unrighteous leaders, followed by the prophecy of the mountain of the Lord's house exalted above the hills in the last days.
[^305]: Clean Tuesday Vespers: Stichera on *Lord, I have cried*, Tone 2, meditating upon the bitter eating in Eden and the restoration wrought by Christ's Cross, gall, and vinegar; followed by the second Genesis reading (Gen. 1:14–23) on the fourth and fifth days of creation (the sun, moon, and stars; fish, sea creatures, and birds).
[^306]: Clean Tuesday Vespers: The second reading from Proverbs (Prov. 1:20–33), in which divine Wisdom cries aloud in the public squares, warning fools and mockers of swift calamity while promising security to the obedient; followed by the Aposticha sticheron, Tone 8 (*Let us accomplish not only abstinence from foods, but estrangement from every material passion*).
[^307]: Tuesday of the First Week at Great Compline (*на повече́рїи вели́комъ*): The Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 2), Ode 1, Tone 6, with troparia examining the murderous envy of Cain, the righteousness of Abel, and the fatherly mercy of God receiving the repentant prodigal.
[^308]: Great Canon, Ode 2, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak" (Deut. 32:1), meditating upon the coats of skins (Gen. 3:21), the garment of shame woven by the passions, the woman who was a sinner anointing Christ with tears (Luke 7:38), and the Publican's cry for justification.
[^309]: Great Canon, Ode 3, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Establish, O Lord, my wavering heart upon the rock of Thy commandments", with troparia recalling the carnal corruption of those in the days of Noah, the mockery of Ham exposing his father's nakedness, and the soul's imperative flight like Lot from the burning of Sodom and Gomorrah.
[^310]: Great Canon, Ode 4, Tone 6, Heirmos: "The prophet heard of Thy coming, O Lord", reflecting on Jacob's ladder of divine ascent, the twelve patriarchs as steps of virtue, the rejection of Esau (*Edom*) selling his birthright for a single mess of pottage, and the exemplary patience of Job upon the dunghill.
[^311]: Great Canon, Ode 5, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Early out of the night waking early", meditating on Moses preserved in the ark upon the Nile, slaying the Egyptian taskmaster, fleeing into the Midian wilderness, beholding the Burning Bush, and the contrasting profane fire offered by Hophni and Phinehas (1 Sam. 2:12–17).
[^312]: Great Canon, Ode 6, Tone 6, Heirmos: "I cried with my whole heart unto the merciful God", recalling Israel's sinful longing for the fleshpots of Egypt over the heavenly manna, Moses striking the rock as a type of Christ's pierced side, Joshua exploring the Promised Land, and the penitential Kontakion: "My soul, my soul, arise! Why sleepest thou?".
[^313]: Great Canon, Ode 7, Tone 6, Heirmos: "We have sinned, we have transgressed", with troparia warning against the irreverence of Uzzah (*За́нъ* / *Оза*, 2 Sam. 6:6–7) touching the Ark, the unnatural rebellion and incest of Absalom against David (2 Sam. 16:21–22), and the deceitful counsel of Ahithophel confounded by Christ.
[^314]: Clean Tuesday at Great Compline (*на повече́рїи вели́комъ*): The Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 2), Ode 8, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Him Whom the heavenly hosts glorify" (*Е҆го́же вѡ́инства нб҃наѧ сла́вѧтъ*), recalling the leprosy of Uzziah (2 Chron. 26:16–21), the repentance of the Ninevites in sackcloth and ashes (Jonah 3:5–10), Jeremiah weeping in the pit of mire for the destruction of Zion (Jer. 38:6; Lam. 1:1), Jonah fleeing to Tarshish, Daniel closing the mouths of lions (Dan. 6:16–23), and the Three Holy Children quenching the fiery furnace (Dan. 3:19–25).
[^315]: Great Canon, Ode 9, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Seedless conception" (*Безсѣ́меннагѡ зача́тїѧ*), with troparia contemplating the temptations of Christ in the wilderness (Matt. 4:1–11), John the Baptist crying in the wilderness and warning Herod of his unlawful marriage to Herodias (Matt. 14:3–12), Christ blessing marriage at Cana of Galilee (John 2:1–11), healing the paralytic carrying his bed (Mark 2:1–12), raising the widow's son at Nain (Luke 7:11–17), healing the centurion's servant (Matt. 8:5–13), conversing with the Samaritan woman (John 4:5–26), healing the woman with the flow of blood (Luke 8:43–48), cleansing lepers, and restoring sight to the blind and strength to the lame.
[^316]: Wednesday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (*Въ сре́дꙋ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀ на оу҆́трени*): Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) following the second and third poetic stichologies of the Psalter, Tone 2, Model: *The noble Joseph* (*Бл҃гообра́зный І҆ѡ́сифъ*), *Being full of mercy* (*Милосе́рдїѧ сꙋ́щи*), *Life-creating* (*Животворѧ́щїй*), and *Doors of mercy* (*Млⷭ҇рдїѧ двє́ри*); followed by the patristic reading from the *Lausiac History* of Palladius (*въ Лаѵсаі́цѣ*) and the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, Ode 3, Tone 2, Heirmos: "The desert hath blossomed" (*Процвѣла̀ є҆́сть пꙋсты́нѧ*), meditating upon Christ's voluntary tasting of gall and the destruction of sin through His Cross.
[^317]: Clean Wednesday Matins: Triodion Canon, Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: "Once in Babylon the fiery furnace" (*Пе́щь и҆ногда̀ ѻ҆́гненнаѧ въ Вавѷлѡ́нѣ*), and the second Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Theodore the Studite, Heirmos: "In the bush to Moses" (*Въ кꙋпинѣ̀ Мѡѷсе́ю*), extolling the life-giving stream poured from the Savior's pierced ribs and summoning all creation to bless Christ hanging upon the Tree between the thieves.
[^318]: Clean Wednesday Matins: Triodion Canon, Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: "The unoriginate Father" (*Безнача́льна Роди́телѧ*), heralding the Fast shining brighter than the sun, and the second Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Theodore, Heirmos: "Pure and most pure Mother and Virgin" (*Чи́стꙋю и҆ пречи́стꙋю*), worshipping Christ's Cross, reed, nails, spear, and life-creating Sufferings.
[^319]: Clean Wednesday at the Sixth Hour (*На ѕ҃-мъ часѣ̀*): Troparion of prophecy, Tone 4 (*Thou knowest our frame, Thou knowest our weakness*, Ps. 102:14), Prokeimenon from Psalm 9 (*I will confess Thee, O Lord, with my whole heart*), and the prophecy of Isaiah (Isa. 2:2–11) regarding the exaltation of the mountain of the Lord's house, universal peace when nations beat their swords into plowshares, the denunciation of idolatrous riches and divination in the house of Jacob, and the humbling of human arrogance before the terrifying glory of the Lord.
[^320]: Wednesday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Vespers with the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts (*Въ сре́дꙋ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀ ве́чера, є҆гда̀ быва́етъ Преждеѡсвѧще́ннаѧ*): Rubrics prescribing small litanies by the priest at each antiphon of the Psalter stichology, ten stichera on *Lord, I have cried* beginning with the Idiomelon of the day, Tone 8 ("Fasting bodily, brethren, let us fast also spiritually...", Isa. 58:6–7), the Martyrikon (*мече́мъ бо преклони́ша вы̑и*), and prosomoia by St. Joseph (Tone 2, *As lightning, O Christ, sending the apostles as rays of light...*, and *Ascending upon the divine chariot through virtues, Elijah shone forth through fasting...*) and St. Theodore (Tone 5, *Divine apostles, fervent intercessors for the world...*).
[^321]: Clean Wednesday Vespers: Stichera conclusion from the Menaion, the Entrance with the censer, *O Radiant Light* (*Свѣ́те ти́хїй*), the first Prokeimenon from Psalm 11 (*Thou, O Lord, shalt keep us*), followed by the first Genesis reading (Gen. 1:24–2:3) recounting the creation of beasts, cattle, creeping things, the creation of man in the image and likeness of God, the dominion of man, and the hallowing of the seventh day of rest; concluded by the second Prokeimenon from Psalm 12 (*Look upon me and hear me, O Lord my God*), the rubrical blessing with censer and candle (*The Light of Christ illumines all*), and the opening of the Proverbs reading (Prov. 2:1–5).
[^322]: Clean Wednesday Vespers and Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts: The reading from Proverbs (Prov. 2:6–22) detailing the Lord granting wisdom, guarding the paths of the just, and uprooting the wicked from the earth; followed by the solemn chanting of *Let my prayer arise* (Ps. 140:2) with kneeling prostrations by the choirs and people, the Entrance of the Presanctified Gifts (*Now the celestial powers...*) during which all fall prostrate in divine adoration (*поклоне́нїе бг҃оподо́бное*) before Christ truly present in the consecrated Gifts, the Kinonikon (*Taste and see that the Lord is good*, Ps. 33:9), the distribution of antidoron, and the final dismissal.
[^323]: Wednesday of the First Week at Great Compline (*Въ сре́дꙋ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀ на повече́рїи*): Preceded by rubrics for ordinary Lenten Vespers when no Presanctified Liturgy is celebrated and the strict fasting decree (xerophagy on Wednesday evening, complete abstinence from food on Thursday until Friday evening); followed by the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3), Ode 1, Tone 6, Heirmos: "A helper and a protector" (*Помо́щникъ и҆ покрови́тель*), with three prostrations (*мета́нїа г҃*) at each troparion, crying out: "Have mercy on me, O God, have mercy on me", meditating upon the parable of the Prodigal Son (Luke 15:11–32) and the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37).
[^324]: Clean Wednesday at Great Compline (*на повече́рїи вели́комъ*): The Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3), Ode 2, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak" (*Вонмѝ нб҃о, и҆ возглаго́лю*), followed by the second Ode 2, Heirmos: "See, see, that I am God" (*Ви́дите ви́дите, ꙗ҆́кѡ а҆́зъ є҆́смь Бг҃ъ*), and Ode 3 opening (*Оу҆твердѝ Гдⷭ҇и на ка́мени*), contemplating David's fall and tears (2 Sam. 12), the lost beauty of Eden, Cain's fratricide and Lamech's murder (Gen. 4:8, 23), Seth, Enos, Enoch's translation (Gen. 5:24), Noah and the deluge (Gen. 6–7), the repentance of St. Mary of Egypt in the Transjordan desert, and the blessing of Shem and Japhs (Gen. 9:26–27).
[^325]: Clean Wednesday at Great Compline: The Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3), Ode 3 conclusion and Ode 4, Tone 6, Heirmos: "The prophet heard of Thy coming" (*Оу҆слы́ша проро́къ*), meditating upon Abraham leaving Haran and entertaining Angels at the oak of Mamre (Gen. 12:1–4; 18:1–8), the offering of Isaac on Mount Moriah (Gen. 22:1–14), the casting out of Ishmael and Hagar (Gen. 21:9–14), Christ shedding His Blood and water from His pierced side as font and chalice for the Church (John 19:34), the voluntary crucifixion opening Eden, and the parable of the foolish virgins excluded from the heavenly bridal chamber (Matt. 25:1–13).
[^326]: Clean Wednesday at Great Compline: The Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3), Ode 5, Tone 6, Heirmos: "From night early morning seeking Thee" (*Ѿ но́щи ᲂу҆́тренююща*), and Ode 6, Tone 6, Heirmos: "I cried aloud with my whole heart" (*Возопи́хъ всѣ́мъ се́рдцемъ*), recalling Pharaoh's magicians Jannes and Jambres (2 Tim. 3:8; Exod. 7:11), the washing of the soul in tears, Christ's bodily miracles (cleansing lepers, healing the paralytic and the woman with an issue of blood, Mark 2; 5), Joshua conquering Amalek and making a covenant with the Gibeonites (Josh. 8–10), the ark crossing the Jordan into the Promised Land (Josh. 3), and Peter sinking in the sea (Matt. 14:28–31).
[^327]: Clean Wednesday at Great Compline: The Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3), Kontakion, Tone 6: "My soul, arise, why sleepest thou?" (*Дꙋшѐ моѧ̀ воста́ни*), followed by Ode 7, Heirmos: "We have sinned, we have committed iniquity" (*Согрѣши́хомъ, беззако́нновахомъ*), and Ode 8, Heirmos: "Him Whom the heavenly hosts glorify" (*Е҆го́же вѡ́инства нб҃наѧ сла́вѧтъ*), lamenting the idolatrous sins of Manasseh (2 Chron. 33:1–13) and Ahab (1 Kings 21), Elijah shutting the heavens and being fed by the widow of Zarephath (1 Kings 17:1–16), destroying the prophets of Baal and the two captains of fifty (1 Kings 18; 2 Kings 1:9–12), and crying out with the penitent thief, weeping Peter, the publican, the sinful woman, the Canaanite woman, and Martha and Mary over Lazarus (Luke 23:42; 22:62; 18:13; 7:37–50; Matt. 15:22–28; John 11).
[^328]: Clean Wednesday at Great Compline: The Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Part 3 conclusion), Ode 9, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Seedless conception" (*Безсѣ́меннагѡ зача́тїѧ*), with troparia extolling Christ healing all diseases and raising Jairus' daughter (Mark 5:21–43), the salvation of the publican Zacchaeus (Luke 19:1–10) in contrast to Simon the Pharisee, the sinful woman anointing the Savior's feet with an alabaster jar of myrrh and wiping them with her hair (Luke 7:36–50), the condemnation of Chorazin, Bethsaida, and Capernaum down to Hades (Matt. 11:20–24), the faith of the Syrophoenician woman, and the final petition to St. Andrew of Crete, Archbishop of Gortyna (*па́стырю Кри́тскїй*); followed by rubrics for concluding Great Compline.
[^329]: Thursday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (*Въ четверто́къ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ на ᲂу҆́трени*): Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) following the Psalter stichologies, Tone 2, Model: *Having made wiser* (*Оу҆мꙋдри́вый па́че*), and Tone 5, Model: *Brighter than the Cherubim* (*Свѣтлѣ́йшаѧ Херꙋві́мъ*), followed by the Triodion Canons by St. Joseph the Hymnographer and St. Theodore the Studite, Ode 4, Tone 2 and Tone 5, Heirmoi: "Thou hast come from the Virgin" (*Прише́лъ є҆сѝ ѿ Дв҃ы*) and "The works of Thy dispensation, O Lord" (*Дѣла̀ смотре́нїѧ твоегѡ̀ Гдⷭ҇и*), extolling the twelve apostles as luminaries of the Sun of righteousness illuminating the world and guiding the faithful through the Forty-Day Fast.
[^330]: Clean Thursday Matins: Triodion Canons, Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: "Unto the tyrant's decree" (*Велѣ́нїю мꙋчи́телевꙋ*), and the second Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Theodore, Heirmos: "Thy venerable children" (*Преподо́бнїи твоѝ ѻ҆́троцы*), summoning the faithful to fast from all pleasure, ascend the chariot of divine virtues, and implore the holy apostles when they sit upon twelve thrones to judge the tribes of Israel with Christ (Matt. 19:28).
[^331]: Clean Thursday Matins: Triodion Canons, Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: "The uncontainable God" (*Невмѣсти́маго Бг҃а*), and the second Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Theodore, Heirmos: "We magnify, O Christ, Thy pure and all-immaculate Mother" (*Велича́емъ Хрⷭ҇тѐ*), praying to be delivered from the deep abyss of pleasures and to venerate Christ's holy Resurrection and Passion; followed by the Photagogicon and the Aposticha Idiomelon of the day, Tone 3 ("O Lord, Thou hast appointed repentance for me, a sinner...", Ps. 102:10; 2 Pet. 3:9), the Martyrikon, and Theotokion.
[^332]: Clean Thursday at the Sixth Hour (*На ѕ҃-мъ часѣ̀*): Troparion of prophecy, Tone 1 (*Deliver us from enemies visible and invisible, O Lord*, Ps. 78:9–10), the first Prokeimenon from Psalm 13 (*When the Lord turns back the captivity of His people*), followed by the prophecy of Isaiah (Isa. 2:11–21) proclaiming the Day of the Lord of Hosts humbling all human pride, tall cedars of Lebanon, oaks of Bashan, high towers, fortified walls, and merchant ships of Tarshish, when idolaters shall cast their gold and silver images to the bats and hide in rocky clefts; concluded by the second Prokeimenon from Psalm 14 (*Lord, who shall abide in Thy dwelling-place?*).
[^333]: Thursday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Vespers (*Въ четверто́къ а҃. седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ ве́чера*): Rubrics for ordinary Lenten Vespers (when no Presanctified Liturgy is prescribed), ten stichera on *Lord, I have cried* including prosomoia by St. Joseph (Tone 2, Model: *Our Enlightener*, "Illumine me who am darkened by the deceits of hostility...", and "As a Vine hanging upon the Tree, O Savior...") and St. Theodore (Tone 2, Model: *O the great mystery*, "O the power of Thy Cross! It blossomed forth abstinence for the Church..."), Prokeimenon from Psalm 15 (*I will bless the Lord Who hath given me understanding*), and the second Genesis reading (Gen. 2:4–9) recounting the creation of heaven and earth, the mist watering the ground, the formation of Adam from the dust of the earth, the breathing of the breath of life, and the planting of the Garden of Eden with the Tree of Life and the Tree of Knowledge.
[^334]: Thursday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Vespers (*Въ четверто́къ а҃. седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ ве́чера*): Physical leaf p131 (Printed fol. 127). Conclusion of the second Old Testament lesson from Genesis (Gen. 2:10–19), recounting the four rivers flowing from Eden (Pishon, Gihon, Tigris, Euphrates), the Lord placing Adam in the Garden of Eden to till and keep it, the divine prohibition against eating of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil upon pain of death, and the creation of animals brought to Adam for naming; followed by the second Prokeimenon from Psalm 16 (Ps. 16:8, *Keep me, O Lord, as the apple of Thine eye*); the third Old Testament lesson from Proverbs (Prov. 3:1–18) delivering paternal wisdom on keeping God's commandments, trusting in the Lord with all one's heart, honoring Him with firstfruits, enduring divine chastening, and exalting divine Wisdom as more precious than jewels and a tree of life unto all who hold her fast; concluded by the Aposticha Idiomelon of the day, Tone 1 (*Desiring to partake of the divine Pascha, not that which comes from Egypt, but that which comes forth from Zion...*), comparing the spiritual discipline of Great Lent to the Passover preparation of Israel (Exod. 12:11), girding loins with mortification and shodding feet with the sandals of spiritual vigil.
[^335]: Thursday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Great Compline (*Въ четверто́къ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ на повече́рїи*): Physical leaf p132 (Printed fol. 128). Conclusion of Clean Thursday Vespers with the Martyrikon and the Cross-Theotokion, Tone 1, Model: *As one valiant among martyrs* (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ); followed by the fourth and concluding section of the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (*Вели́кїй Канѡ́нъ преподо́бнагѡ ѻ҆ц҃а̀ на́шегѡ А҆ндре́а Кри́тскагѡ и҆ И҆ерꙋсали́мскагѡ*), Tone 6, Ode 1, Heirmos: *A Helper and a Protector* (*Помо́щникъ и҆ покрови́тель*, Exod. 15:2), sung twice. The rubrics prescribe three prostrations (*покло́ны г҃*) at each troparion, accompanied by the penitential refrain: *Have mercy on me, O God, have mercy on me* (*Поми́лꙋй мѧ̀ Бж҃е, поми́лꙋй мѧ̀*). The opening troparia address Christ as the Lamb of God (John 1:29) and beseech the gift of compunction (*сле́зы ᲂу҆миле́нїѧ*), confessing the squandering of the soul's divine substance in sins, and turning in the final stanza to St. Mary of Egypt (*Марі́и*).
[^336]: Great Canon Part 4, Odes 2 and 3, Tone 6: Physical leaf p133 (Printed fol. 129). Ode 2, Heirmos: *See, see, that I am God* (*Ви́дите ви́дите, ꙗ҆́кѡ а҆́зъ є҆́смь Бг҃ъ*, Deut. 32:39), meditating on Lamech's song of violence (Gen. 4:23), the pride of the builders of the Tower of Babel (Gen. 11:4), the rain of fire and brimstone upon Sodom (Gen. 19:24), and invoking St. Mary of Egypt's prayer in the abyss of evils; followed by Ode 3, Heirmos: *Establish, O Lord, upon the rock* (*Оу҆твердѝ Гдⷭ҇и на ка́мени*, 1 Sam. 2:2), reproving the soul for bringing forth the insolence of Ishmael through voluntary enslavement to passion (Gen. 16:1–16), urging it to mount Jacob's ladder of virtues (Gen. 28:12), to emulate Melchizedek the righteous king-priest (Gen. 14:18; Heb. 7:1–3), and warning against looking back like Lot's wife becoming a pillar of salt (Gen. 19:26).
[^337]: Great Canon Part 4, Ode 4, Tone 6: Physical leaf p134 (Printed fol. 130). Conclusion of Ode 3 and Ode 4, Heirmos: *The Prophet heard of Thine advent, O Lord, and was afraid* (*Оу҆слы́ша проро́къ*, Hab. 3:2). St. Andrew reflects on the sudden ruin, disease, and poverty that befell the blameless and wealthy Patriarch Job (Job 1:1–22; 2:1–10), asking how the sinful soul will endure divine judgment if even the righteous suffered such adversity; prays against the boastfulness of the Pharisee while imploring the contrite humility of the Publican (Luke 18:10–14); mourns the defilement of the bodily temple and neglect of the divine Law; and praises St. Mary of Egypt's extraordinary conversion from the depths of sin to a life astonishing to angelic nature.
[^338]: Great Canon Part 4, Odes 5 and 6, Tone 6, with Kontakion: Physical leaf p135 (Printed fol. 131). Ode 5, Heirmos: *Watching early from the night* (*Ѿ но́щи ᲂу҆́треннююща*, Isa. 26:9), urging the soul to emulate the bowed-down woman whom Christ straightened (Luke 13:11–13), to thirst for the living water revealed to the Samaritan woman at the well of Sychar (John 4:7–14), and to wash spiritual eyes in the spiritual pool of Siloam (John 9:7); Ode 6, Heirmos: *I cried with my whole heart* (*Возопи́хъ всѣ́мъ се́рдцемъ*, Ps. 118:145; Jonah 2:2), presenting the repentant soul as the lost coin of the Gospel (Luke 15:8–9) and calling for warfare against the passions like Joshua against Amalek and the deceptive Gibeonites (Josh. 8–10); concluded by the Kontakion of the Great Canon, Tone 6 (*My soul, my soul, arise! Why sleepest thou? The end draws near...*), composed by St. Romanos the Melodist.
[^339]: Great Canon Part 4, Odes 7 and 8, Tone 6: Physical leaf p136 (Printed fol. 132). Ode 7, Heirmos: *We have sinned, we have transgressed* (*Согрѣши́хомъ*, Dan. 9:5), where the soul weeps like King Hezekiah upon his sickbed seeking an extension of life (Isa. 38:1–5; 2 Kgs. 20:1–6), confesses transgressions exceeding the sinful woman in the Gospel (Luke 7:37–38), and pleads for renewal according to the penitential prayer of David (Ps. 50:12); Ode 8, Heirmos: *Him Whom the hosts of heaven glorify* (*Е҆го́же во́инства*, Dan. 3:52–90), pouring out tears as an alabaster vessel of myrrh upon Christ's feet, entreating the Good Shepherd to recover His lost sheep (Luke 15:4–7), and shuddering before the intolerable judgment seat and fiery furnace of the Last Judgment (Matt. 25:31–46).
[^340]: Great Canon Part 4, Ode 9, Tone 6: Physical leaf p137 (Printed fol. 133). Conclusion of Ode 8 with the commemoration of St. Zosimas beholding St. Mary of Egypt as an angel in the flesh; followed by Ode 9, Heirmos: *Incorruptible Conception* (*Безсѣ́меннагѡ Зача́тїѧ*, Luke 1:46–55), invoking the Son of David to heal demonic afflictions and grant the repentant thief's promise (*Amen, I say unto thee: Today shalt thou be with Me in Paradise*, Luke 23:42–43), meditating upon the convulsions of nature during the Crucifixion (Matt. 27:51–54), offering spiritual poverty as an acceptable sacrifice, venerating St. Mary of Egypt crossing the Jordan upon the waters (cf. Life of St. Mary by St. Sophronius), and invoking the intercession of the canon's author, St. Andrew of Crete (*А҆ндре́е честны́й, и҆ ѻ҆́ч҃е требл҃же́ннѣйшїй, па́стырю Кри́тскїй...*).
[^341]: Friday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (*Въ пато́къ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ на ᲂу҆́трени*): Physical leaf p138 (Printed fol. 134). Conclusion of Clean Thursday Compline with the intercessory city-guardian Theotokion (*Гра́дъ тво́й сохранѝ Бгороди́тельнице пречⷭ҇таѧ...*); followed by the major display headpiece opening Matins of Clean Friday, prescribing Trinity hymns, Sessional Hymns of the Cross after the first stichology of the Psalter, Sessional Hymn by St. Joseph after the second stichology (Tone 2, Model: *The noble Joseph* / *Благоѡбра́зный І҆ѡ́сифъ*), and Sessional Hymn by St. Theodore the Studite after the third stichology (Tone 2, Model: *Being the wellspring of loving-kindness* / *Мѵ́рꙋ сꙋ́щи*), with Cross-Theotokia mourning Christ crucified upon the Tree.
[^342]: On the melodic model *Tone 2, Podoben: "Being the wellspring of loving-kindness"* (Slavonic: *Мѵ́рꙋ сꙋ́щи:*): Physical leaf p138 (Printed fol. 134). The printed Slavonic witness *Мѵ́рꙋ сꙋ́щи* is a typographical and recensional contraction for *Милосе́рдїѧ сꙋ́щи и҆сто́чникъ*, directly corresponding to the canonical Byzantine Greek prosomoion incipit *Εὐσπλαγχνίας ὑπάρχουσα πηγή* (Tone 2).
[^343]: Friday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (Canons / Triodia): Physical leaf p139 (Printed fol. 135). Ode 5, Tone 2, by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, Heirmos: *Enlightenment of those who lie in darkness* (*Просвѣще́нїе во тьмѣ̀ лежа́щихъ*, Isa. 9:2; 26:9), lamenting demonic deception, praying for liberation through the Cross, and asking to reach the Holy Resurrection; followed by the second Ode 5, Tone 2, by St. Theodore the Studite, Heirmos: *The night having passed* (*Но́щи преше́дшей*, Rom. 13:12), venerating Christ crucified at Golgotha and exhorting the faithful to suffer mockery and shame with Christ; and Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: *Into the fiery furnace* (*Въ пе́щь ѻ҆́гненнꙋю*, Dan. 3:49–50), warning against the deadly spiritual slumber of inattention.
[^344]: Friday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (Odes 8 and 9): Physical leaf p140 (Printed fol. 136). Ode 8 conclusion and second Ode 8, Tone 2, by St. Theodore the Studite, Heirmos: *In the bush to Moses* (*Въ кꙋпинѣ̀ Мѡѷсе́ю*, Exod. 3:2), praising Christ crowned with thorns and robed in purple (Matt. 27:28–29; John 19:2–5), pouring forth life and incorruption from His pierced side (John 19:34), with a praise of the Holy Cross (*O Cross, scepter of Christ, horn of the Church, victory of kings, keeper of Christians...*); followed by Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Joseph, Heirmos: *He Who before the sun* (*И҆́же пре́жде со́лнца*, Ps. 71:17; 109:3), beseeching Christ to cleanse the leprous soul with His blood, rebuking hypocrisy that fasts from meats while feeding upon malice and negligence (cf. Isa. 58:3–7); and the opening of the second Ode 9, Tone 2, by St. Theodore, Heirmos: *Pure and all-immaculate* (*Чи́стꙋю и҆ пречи́стꙋю*).
[^345]: Friday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Matins (*Въ пѧто́къ а҃. седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ на ᲂу҆́трени*): Physical leaf p141 (Printed fol. 137). Conclusion of the second canon at Ode 9 by St. Theodore the Studite with Trinity troparion, Theotokion, Cross-troparion, and Heirmos (*Pure and all-immaculate Mother and Virgin...*); followed by the Exaposteilarion of the Tone, the Aposticha Idiomelon of the day in Tone 8 (*Let us joyfully receive the bequest of the fast...*), the Martyrikon, and the Cross-Theotokion in Tone 8, Model: *O most marvelous wonder* (*Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ*), meditating on the lament of the Virgin at Golgotha. The rubrics order the conclusion of Matins (*It is good to give praise to the Lord...*), specify that the First Hour is read without kathisma stichology on all Fridays of Great Lent, and provide the Troparion of the Sixth Hour in Tone 5 (*Prevent us with Thy compassions, O Lord...*).
[^346]: Prophecy of Isaiah (Isa. 3:1–14) at the Sixth Hour: Physical leaf p142 (Printed fol. 138). Preceded by the First Prokeimenon from Psalm 17 (Ps. 17:2, *I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength*). The prophetic reading depicts the total collapse of societal leadership and moral order in Jerusalem and Judea as divine chastisement for sin, where the Lord removes warriors, judges, prophets, counselors, and artisans, leaving immature youths to rule, leading to civil strife, lawlessness, and rebellion against God, prophetically prefiguring the unjust condemnation of the Righteous One (*Let us bind the righteous man, for he is cumbersome unto us*, Isa. 3:10; cf. Wis. 2:12), followed by the Second Prokeimenon from Psalm 18 (Ps. 18:15, *O Lord, my Helper and my Redeemer*).
[^347]: Friday of the First Week of the Holy and Great Fast at Vespers with the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts (*Въ пѧто́къ пе́рвыѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ поста̀ ве́чера*): Physical leaf p142 (Printed fol. 138). The introductory rubric directs that any occurring Menaion saint for Saturday or Sunday be transferred to Compline at the discretion of the ecclesiarch; followed by the major display headpiece for Clean Friday Vespers combined with the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, appointing 10 stichera at *Lord, I have cried*: the Idiomelon of the day twice in Tone 5 (*Come, ye faithful, let us work the works of God in the light...*), calling the faithful to active works of mercy and ethical integrity during the fast.
[^348]: Commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Recruit (Teron) at Vespers: Physical leaf p143 (Printed fol. 139). Following four Martyrika of the Oktoechos, four Idiomela are sung in Tone 2 in honor of St. Theodore the Recruit (*Ѳео́дѡръ Тѵ́рѡнъ*), celebrating his steadfastness under torture, his miracles in delivering captives, healing the sick, finding lost property, and rescuing a widow's son on a white horse; followed by the Doxastikon in Tone 6 (*Using the apostate tyrant as his vessel...*), which narrates the foundational miracle of Kolyvo: in 362 A.D., Emperor Julian the Apostate sought to contaminate the food supply of Constantinople with blood from pagan sacrifices during Clean Week, but St. Theodore appeared in a dream to Archbishop Eudoxius, instructing the Christians to boil wheat (*kolyvo*) instead of purchasing defiled market provisions. Concluded by the Entrance without Gospel, *O Gladsome Light*, and the Prokeimenon from Psalm 19 (Ps. 19:2, *May the Lord hear thee in the day of affliction*).
[^349]: Old Testament Reading from Genesis (Gen. 2:20–3:20) at Vespers: Physical leaf p144 (Printed fol. 140). The second reading of Clean Friday Vespers, recounting the naming of the animals by Adam, the creation of Eve from Adam's rib as his suitable helpmeet, the primordial institution of marriage (*and they two shall be one flesh*), the temptation and deception of Eve by the serpent, the Fall of man through partaking of the forbidden tree, the divine confrontation in Eden, the curses pronounced upon the serpent, woman, and man, the Protoevangelium (*He shall watch against thy head, and thou shalt watch against His heel*, Gen. 3:15), the expulsion into labor and mortality (*earth thou art, and unto earth shalt thou go*), and Adam naming his wife Life (*Жи́знь* / Eve); followed by the Second Prokeimenon from Psalm 20 (Ps. 20:14, *Be Thou exalted, O Lord, in Thy strength*).
[^350]: Old Testament Reading from Proverbs (Prov. 3:19–34) at Vespers: Physical leaf p145 (Printed fol. 141). The third lesson of Clean Friday Vespers, extolling divine Wisdom as the cosmic foundation of heaven and earth, counseling the seeker of wisdom to maintain sound judgment, prudence, and peace, assuring divine protection against terrors, commanding immediate charity unto the needy (*Withhold not to do good unto the needy, when thine hand hath power to help*), forbidding malice, strife, and envy toward violent men, and concluding with the classic aphorism of divine justice: *The Lord resists the proud, but gives grace unto the humble* (Prov. 3:34; cf. Jas. 4:6; 1 Pet. 5:5).
[^351]: The Supplicatory Canon to the Holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Recruit and the Setting Forth of Kolyvo: Physical leaf p145 (Printed fol. 141). Following the Prayer behind the Ambo (*По Заамво́нной же мл҃твѣ*) of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, a dish of boiled wheat sweetened with honey and dried fruits (*kolyvo*) is set forth on the Tetrapod; the celebrants chant Psalm 142 (*O Lord, hear my prayer*), *God is the Lord*, the Troparion of St. Theodore in Tone 2 (*Great are the accomplishments of faith! In the fountain of flame, as upon the water of rest...*), the Theotokion (*All beyond comprehension...*), Psalm 50, and begin the Supplicatory Canon.
[^352]: The Supplicatory Canon to St. Theodore by St. John of Damascus, Tone 8 (Odes 1–4): Physical leaf p146 (Printed fol. 142). The acrostic of the canon is explicitly noted in the rubric: *I sing of thee, namesake of God's gifts* (*Бж҃їихъ тѧ̀ пою̀ дарѡ́въ тезоимени́те*, playing on the Greek etymology of Theodore, *Θεόδωρος* = "Gift of God"). St. John of Damascus commemorates the saint's refusal to offer pagan sacrifice, his enlistment as a soldier of Christ, his testimony before the persecutors, and his defense of true doctrine against pagan polytheism.
[^353]: Supplicatory Canon to St. Theodore (Odes 5–7 and Kontakion): Physical leaf p147 (Printed fol. 143). Ode 5 recounts the burning of the pagan temple of Cybele by St. Theodore; Ode 6 reflects on the vanity of the idols and the steadfastness of the martyrs; followed by the Kontakion of St. Theodore in Tone 8 (*Having received the faith of Christ within thy heart as a shield...*); and Ode 7, meditating on the saint's imprisonment and the miraculous visitation of Christ comforting him in his cell as once the three youths in the Babylonian furnace.
[^354]: Rubrics for the Blessing of Kolyvo and Concluding Rites: Physical leaf p148 (Printed fol. 144). Conclusion of the Supplicatory Canon with Odes 8 and 9, Trisagion, Troparion, Kontakion, and Theotokion; followed by the special prayer of blessing over the kolyvo with censing by the priest, *Blessed be the Name of the Lord*, Psalm 33 (*I will bless the Lord at all times*), the distribution of blessed bread (*antidoron*), and the dismissal. An additional rubric outlines the alternate order of the service if the Presanctified Liturgy is not celebrated.
[^355]: Monastic Typikon on Fasting Mitigation and Lenten Refectory: Physical leaf p149 (Printed fol. 145). The rubric records the ancient tradition of the Palestinian Lavras of St. Sabbas the Sanctified and St. Euthymius the Great permitting wine and oil on Clean Friday on account of the solemnity of St. Theodore's feast, while noting that contemporary Ruthenian practice refrains from oil and wine on this day out of reverence for Clean Week, prescribing instead cooked food with prunes without oil or pickles (*armea*, sauerkraut/brine dishes), while those desiring stricter asceticism practice xerophagy (*сꙋхоѧде́нїе*).
[^356]: Ruthenian Synodal Decree on Compline and Memorial Rites: Physical leaf p149 (Printed fol. 145). The rubric explicitly cites the Provincial Synod of Lviv held in 1740 (*Собо́ръ, бы́вшїй въ гра́дѣ Льво́вѣ въ лѣ́то ҂аѱч҃а*), which decreed that in conformity with Greek usage, Small Compline (*повече́рїа ма̑лаѧ*) be celebrated on all Fridays of Great Lent rather than Great Compline, and regulates the singing of the resting canons (*поко́йныѧ канѡ́ны*) in the cemetery crypt chapel (*въ гробни́цѣ*) as well as the distribution of the Saturday and Sunday Menaion canons at Compline.
[^357]: First Saturday of the Holy Fast at Matins (*Въ сꙋббѡ́тꙋ пе́рвꙋю ст҃ы́хъ постѡ́въ, на ᲂу҆́трени*): Physical leaf p149 (Printed fol. 145). The service commemorates the Holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Recruit and his miracle of kolyvo; after the Six Psalms and *God is the Lord* in Tone 2, the Troparion of the saint is sung twice (*Great are the accomplishments of faith...*), followed by Sessional Hymns from the Oktoechos after the first kathisma and two Sessional Hymns of St. Theodore in Tone 3, Model: *Of the divine faith* (*Бж҃їѧ вѣ́ры*), praising the saint for quenching the deception of idols and dispensing healings throughout the world.
[^358]: First Matins Canon of St. Theodore by John Mauropous, Tone 4: Physical leaf p150 (Printed fol. 146). Following the reading of the Saint's Life/Passio (*чте́мъ мꙋче́нїе ст҃а́гѡ*) and Psalm 50, the first canon is appointed on 8, composed by John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita (11th century). The acrostic reads: *Most glorious Tyro, the glory of the first Saturday* (*Тѵ́рѡнъ пресла́вный, сла́ва пе́рвѣй сꙋббѡ́тѣ*), with an additional acrostic in the opening troparia. Ode 1 begins with a praise of the Theotokos before extolling the fame of St. Theodore across the universe.
[^359]: Second Matins Canon of St. Theodore by John Mauropous, Tone 6: Physical leaf p150 (Printed fol. 146). The second canon in honor of the saint for Matins, also composed by John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita, in Tone 6, Ode 1, Heirmos: *Traversing the deep as on dry land* (*Ꙗ҆́кѡ по сꙋ́хꙋ*), opening with a prayer for divine eloquence to praise the martyr's wonders, celebrating his steadfast confession, and condemning Julian the Apostate's betrayal of the Christian faith.
[^360]: First Saturday of the Holy Fast at Matins: Ode 3 of the Matins Canons of St. Theodore the Recruit (*Въ сꙋббѡ́тꙋ пе́рвꙋю ст҃ы́хъ постѡ́въ, на ᲂу҆́трени, пѣ́снь г҃*): Physical leaf p151 (Printed fol. 147). Following Ode 1, the service continues with Ode 3 of the two canons composed by John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita (11th century). The first canon is sung with the Heirmos *Thy hymn-singers, O Theotokos* (*Твоѧ̑ пѣсносло́вцы*), followed by five troparia praising the martyr's courage and a Theotokion. The second canon is sung with the Heirmos *There is none holy like Thee* (*Нѣ́сть ст҃ъ*), followed by troparia, Glory, Both now, the Katavasia of Ode 3, and the Sessional Hymn of St. Theodore in Tone 8, Model: *Of the Wisdom and Word* (*Премꙋ́дрости сло́ва*), which extols the martyr's spiritual armor and his burning of the pagan idol shrine.
[^361]: Matins Canon of St. Theodore: Ode 4 (*Пѣ́снь д҃*): Physical leaf p152 (Printed fol. 148). The leaf opens with the second Sessional Hymn of the saint in Tone 8, Model: *Of the Wisdom and Word*, recounting his trial before Maximian and his unharmed passage through fire, followed by the Sessional Theotokion. Ode 4 follows with the Heirmos of the first canon *He Who sits in glory* (*Сѣдѧ́й въ сла́вѣ*), meditating on the incarnation of the God-Man and the uncrushed bones of the martyr in his fiery sacrifice; and the second canon with Heirmos *Christ is my power* (*Хрⷭ҇то́съ моѧ̀ си́ла*), recounting Julian the Apostate's wicked decree to secretly sprinkle market provisions with idol sacrifice blood during Clean Week, and the gathering of the faithful in the holy temple of God upon discovering the plot; concluding with the Katavasia of Ode 4.
[^362]: Matins Canon of St. Theodore: Odes 5 and 6 (*Пѣ́снь є҃ и҆ ѕ҃*): Physical leaf p153 (Printed fol. 149). Ode 5 begins with the Heirmos of the first canon *All things were amazed* (*Оу҆жасо́шасѧ всѧ́чєскаѧ*), celebrating the opening of the portals of grace and the living presence of the martyr defending the Orthodox from the snares of the enemy, and the second canon with Heirmos *By Thy divine light, O Good One* (*Бж҃їимъ свѣ́томъ твои́мъ*), recounting Archbishop Eudoxius's terror and his tearful all-night prayers to God, and the dispatch of St. Theodore from heaven to instruct the pastor; concluding with the Katavasia of Ode 5. Ode 6 follows with the Heirmoi *This divine and all-honorable feast* (*Бж҃е́ственное сїѐ и҆ всечестно́е*) and *The sea of life* (*Жите́йское мо́ре*), depicting the martyr's dialogue with the archbishop.
[^363]: Kontakion, Ikos, and Synaxarion of Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos for the Miracle of the Kolyvo: Physical leaf p154 (Printed fol. 150). Following Ode 6, the Kontakion in Tone 8 (*Having received the faith of Christ within thy heart as a shield...*) and Ikos are appointed, followed by the full historical Synaxarion composed by Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335). The Synaxarion details the historical context of Julian the Apostate's reign (361–363 A.D.), who, seeking to subtly contaminate the Christians during their holy fast in Clean Week, ordered the Prefect of Constantinople to remove all standard provisions from the market and substitute food and drink mingled with blood from pagan sacrifices. The all-seeing Eye of God dispatched the Great-Martyr Theodore the Recruit (from the cohort of the Tyrones) to appear not in a dream but visibly in bodily form to Archbishop Eudoxius, commanding him to warn the Christian flock and provide boiled wheat (*kolyvo*, according to the Euchaita dialect) to sustain the faithful.
[^364]: Conclusion of the Synaxarion and Matins Canon: Odes 7 and 8 (*Пѣ́снь з҃ и҆ и҃*): Physical leaf p155 (Printed fol. 151). The leaf opens with the conclusion of Xanthopoulos's Synaxarion, narrating the defeat and shame of Julian the Apostate, the restoration of clean market provisions, and the institution of the annual Saturday kolyvo memorial; followed by a summary of St. Theodore's original martyrdom under Maximian (c. 306 A.D.) in Amasea of Pontus, his burning of the temple of Cybele, and his entry into the fiery furnace where he yielded his soul unto God. Ode 7 follows with Heirmoi *The godly-minded youths did not serve the creature* (*Не послꙋжи́ша тва́ри*) and *O Thou Who didst make the furnace dewy* (*Росода́тельнꙋ ᲂу҆́бѡ пе́щь*), shaming Julian and exalting Christ the King; followed by the opening strophes of Ode 8 with Heirmos *O Redeemer of all* (*И҆зба́вителю всѣ́хъ*), sanctifying Saturday as the memorial of great salvation.
[^365]: Matins Canon of St. Theodore: Conclusion of Ode 8 and Ode 9 (*Пѣ́снь и҃ и҆ ѳ҃*): Physical leaf p156 (Printed fol. 152). Ode 8 concludes with the troparia recounting the deliverance from the defiled banquet, the second canon with Heirmos *Out of the flame for the venerable ones* (*И҆з̾ пла́мене преподо́бнымъ*), and the Katavasia of the Three Youths (*The childbearing of the Theotokos saved the godly youths in the furnace...*). Ode 9 follows with the Heirmos of the first canon *Thy childbearing was incorrupt* (*Ржⷭ҇тво̀ твоѐ нетлѣ́нно*), bidding the Church of Christ to take courage against her adversaries through the advocacy of her martyrs; and the second canon with Heirmos *For man to see God is impossible* (*Бг҃а человѣ́кѡмъ не возмо́жно*), extolling St. Theodore as the radiant defender of all who keep his eternal memory.
[^366]: Conclusion of Ode 9, Exapostilarion, and Praises of St. Theodore (*На хвали́техъ*): Physical leaf p157 (Printed fol. 153). The leaf concludes Ode 9 with the troparia, Glory, Both now, and the Katavasia *Let every earthborn mortal leap for joy* (*Вся́къ земноро́дный*); followed by the Exapostilarion of St. Theodore in Tone 2, Model: *With the disciples* (*Со ᲂу҆чени́ки*), and its Theotokion. At the Praises (*На хвали́техъ*), four Stichera are appointed in Tone 1, Model: *Of the celestial orders* (*Нбⷭ҇ныхъ чинѡ́въ*), calling the faithful to gather in harmony and celebrate the memory of St. Theodore the Recruit (*Тѵ́рѡнъ*), honoring his endurance, spiritual riches, and heavenly gifts.
[^367]: Praises Doxastikon and Aposticha of St. Theodore (*На стїхо́внѣ*): Physical leaf p158 (Printed fol. 154). The Praises conclude with the Doxastikon in Tone 6 (*A gift of sanctification and wealth of divine life...*), followed by the Theotokion *Thou art the true vine* (*Бцⷣе, ты̀ є҆сѝ лоза̀ и҆́стиннаѧ*). At the Aposticha, three Idiomelon Stichera are sung in Tone 2 (*Ликꙋ́ютъ собо́ры мꙋ́ченикѡвъ...*), set to Psalm verses (Ps. 91:13 and Ps. 91:14), extolling the clean fast combined with the miracles of the martyrs, by which the faithful are cleansed from spiritual defilement; followed by the Doxastikon in Tone 8 (*Having devoutly armed thyself with suffering courage...*) and the concluding Theotokion *O Sovereign Lady, receive the prayers of Thy servants*.
[^368]: Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom and Monastic Typikon on Fasting Mitigations: Physical leaf p159 (Printed fol. 155). The rubrics appoint the First Hour joined to Matins. At the Divine Liturgy, the Typika and Beatitudes are sung with Odes 3 and 6 of the Saint's Canon; Prokeimenon in Tone 7 (Ps. 63:11, *The righteous man shall be glad in the Lord*); Epistle readings for the day (Heb. 3:12–16, pericope 313) and for the Saint (2 Tim. 2:1–10, pericope 292); Alleluia in Tone 4 (Ps. 91:13); Gospel lessons for the day (Mark 1:35–44, pericope 7) and for the Saint (John 15:17–16:2, pericope 52); and Communion Hymn (Ps. 111:6). The Trapeza rubric orders the reading of the Encomium of St. Theodore by St. Gregory of Nyssa (*In Praise of the Great Martyr Theodore*), prescribing cooked beans, white and black olives, cooked food with oil, and kolyvo, with wine permitted in moderation (*по крагово́лю*) for the feast, according to the ancient Palestinian rule of the Lavras of St. Sabbas the Sanctified and St. Euthymius the Great; concluding with the general Lenten statute that fish is permitted only twice during the Great Fast: on the Feast of the Annunciation (March 25) and on Palm Sunday (Flower-bearing Sunday). Below this text is a detailed woodcut engraving representing Faith as an allegorical crowned maiden seated upon clouds, bearing the Cross and palm branch.
[^369]: First Sunday of the Great Fast (Sunday of the Triumph of Orthodoxy) at Little Vespers (*Недѣ́лѧ пе́рваѧ ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀, въ ню́же Правосла́вїе воспомина́етсѧ, на ма́лѣй вече́рни*): Physical leaf p160 (Printed fol. 156). Display headpiece featuring a rectangular woodcut engraving of the Holy Eucharist—a chalice surmounted by a crucifix and supported by open Scriptures, emitting radiant rays of divine light within an ornamental foliate frame. The rubrics appoint the customary All-Night Vigil (*Бдѣ́нїе ѻ҆бы́чное*), beginning on Saturday evening with Little Vespers, setting four Stichera in Tone 1, Model: *Of the celestial orders* (*Нбⷭ҇ныхъ чинѡ́въ*), composed by Nicholas. The stichera celebrate the restoration of holy icons in Constantinople on the First Sunday of Lent in 843 A.D. under Empress Theodora and Patriarch Methodius, explicitly invoking the theological definition of St. Basil the Great from *De Spiritu Sancto* 18.45 (*The honor rendered unto the image passes unto the Prototype*); concluded by the Doxastikon-Theotokion in Tone 1, Model: *Of the celestial orders*, extolling the mystery of the Incarnation whereby the invisible God became visible man through the pure blood of the Virgin, permitting the veneration of His bodily image.
[^370]: First Sunday of the Great Fast (Sunday of Orthodoxy) at Little Vespers conclusion: Physical leaf p161 (Printed fol. 157). The Aposticha stichera (Tone 2, Podoben: *O House of Ephratha* / *До́ме Еѵфра́ѳовъ*) celebrate the triumph of Orthodoxy over five major historical heresies and their champions explicitly named in the second troparion: Arius (Arianism, 1st Ecumenical Council), Macedonius I of Constantinople (Pneumatomachianism, 2nd Ecumenical Council), Peter the Fuller of Antioch (Monophysitism / Theopaschism), Severus of Antioch (Monophysitism), and Pyrrhus of Constantinople (Monothelitism, 6th Ecumenical Council). The third troparion commemorates Empress Theodora (c. 815–867 A.D.), who as regent for her infant son Michael III orchestrated the Council of Constantinople in 843 A.D., giving "the rod of strength" unto the Orthodox. The service concludes with the dismissal troparion *Пречи́стомꙋ твоемꙋ̀ ѻ҆́бразꙋ* ("Thy pure image do we venerate").
[^371]: Sunday of the Triumph of Orthodoxy at Great Vespers (*На вели́цѣй вече́рни*): Physical leaf p161 (Printed fol. 157). Display headpiece featuring an ornamental woodcut band. The rubrics appoint the customary kathisma from the Psalter (*Блаже́нъ мꙋ́жъ*), followed at "Lord, I have cried" by 10 stichera: 2 of the Resurrection and 3 Anatolian stichera from the Octoechos in the Tone of the week, and 4 prosomoia from the Triodion in Tone 6, to the melody *Having laid aside all earthly hope* (*Всѐ ѿло́жше* / *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*), authored by St. Theophanes Graptos. The hymnody develops the Christological basis of iconography: the incomprehensible, immaterial Word shone forth before all ages from the Father, but was foretold by the Holy Prophets to become a visible child born of the Virgin, taking upon Himself all human properties, therefore depicting His likeness raises the believer unto divine love.
[^372]: Doxasticon at "Lord, I have cried" (*Сла́ва, гласъ в: Блгдть возсіѧ̀ и҆́стины...*): Physical leaf p162 (Printed fol. 158). This famous hymn synthesizes the dogmatic definition of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787 A.D.), contrasting the Old Testament types and shadows of the Tabernacle with the reality of the Incarnate Word. The text explicitly asserts the theology of veneration: the bodily form of the Savior is "devoutly venerated, not deified" (*блгче́стнѡ покланя́емый, не бг҃отвори́мый* / προσκυνούμενος, οὐ θεοποιούμενος), refuting the iconoclast charge that icon veneration constitutes idolatry.
[^373]: Aposticha Doxasticon at Great Vespers (*Сла́ва, гласъ в: И҆з̾ нече́стіѧ ко блгче́стіе преше́дше...*): Physical leaf p162 (Printed fol. 158). The hymn enjoins the faithful to venerate holy images depicted upon walls, wooden panels, and sacred vessels, citing the classic patristic formula of St. Basil the Great from *De Spiritu Sancto* 18.45: "For the honor of the image passes unto the Prototype" (*че́сть бо ѻ҆́браза, ꙗ҆́коже глаго́летъ Васи́лій, на первоwбра́зное прехо́дитъ* / ἡ γὰρ τῆς εἰκόνος τιμὴ ἐπὶ τὸ πρωτότυπον διαβαίνει). The dismissal troparion appointed for the feast is *Пречи́стомꙋ ѻ҆́бразꙋ твоемꙋ̀ покланя́емся Блгíй* ("Thy pure image do we venerate, O Good One"), which also serves as the chief Festal Troparion at Matins.
[^374]: Matins Canon of the Holy Prophets and Holy Icons: Physical leaf p163 (Printed fol. 159). Authored by St. Theophanes Graptos ("the Branded", c. 778–845 A.D.), Metropolitan of Nicaea, who with his brother St. Theodore suffered imprisonment and facial branding under Emperor Theophilus. The canon bears the alphabetic/metrical acrostic: *Дне́сь блгче́стія прїисквѣ́тлаѧ прїи́де зарѧ̀* (in Greek: *Σήμερον εὐσεβίης θεοφεγγέος ἤλυθεν αἴγλη* — "Today the all-radiant dawn of piety has arrived"). The Katavasiai for the feast are taken from the Canon of the Cross (*Мо́рѧ чермнꙋ́ю пꙋчи́нꙋ*).
[^375]: Historical commemoration of the Imperial Restorers in Ode 1 and Ode 3: Physical leaf p163 (Printed fol. 159). St. Theophanes commemorates the coregency of the pious Empress Theodora and her young son Michael III (*Мїхаи́ла же и҆ Ѳео́дѡры* / *съ бг҃овѣнча́нною Ѽра́слію*), through whose imperial decree and synodical convening in 843 A.D. the Church was restored to peace and unity, and the iconoclast hierarchy deposed.
[^376]: Odes 4 and 5 of the Matins Canon: Physical leaf p164 (Printed fol. 160). Ode 4 develops Habakkuk's prophetic vision of the divine power, celebrating the empress who adorned the churches with icons of Christ and the saints out of longing for the heavenly Kingdom. Ode 5 draws upon Isaiah's morning vigil (*Ты̀ Гд҃и, мо́й свѣ́тъ въ мі́ръ прише́лъ є҆сѝ*), praying that the Church remain forever unshaken by heretical agitation, and that God exalt the horn of Orthodox rulers who honor His image.
[^377]: Kontakion and Ikos of the Sunday of Orthodoxy: Physical leaf p165 (Printed fol. 161). The Kontakion (Tone 6, Automelon: *Неѡпи́санное Сло́во Ѻ҆ц҃ее* / *Ὁ ἀπερίγραπτος Λόγος τοῦ Πατρός*) expresses the fundamental incarnational defense of icon veneration: the uncircumscribed Word of the Father accepted bodily circumscription through His Nativity from the Theotokos, thereby restoring the fallen and defiled image of man to its primordial divine beauty. The Ikos expounds this mystery as the culmination of the prophetic visions, leading to the confession of the Holy Trinity and baptism into Christ.
[^378]: Synaxarion of the Sunday of Orthodoxy (Part 1): Physical leaf p165 (Printed fol. 161). Composed by the Byzantine ecclesiastical historian Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335 A.D.). The narrative opens with elegiac couplets praising the becoming veneration of holy images, then details the outbreak of the First Iconoclasm under Leo III the Isaurian (717–741 A.D.), his deposition of Patriarch St. Germanus I in 730 A.D., and the legendary tale that Leo was seduced into iconoclasm in his youth through a magical pact with Jewish prognosticators in Syria. It outlines the succession through Constantine V Copronymus (741–775 A.D.) and Leo IV the Khazar (775–780 A.D.), leading to the restoration of icons at the Seventh Ecumenical Council (787 A.D.) under Empress Irene and Patriarch St. Tarasius, followed by the brief reigns of Nicephorus I, Stauracius, and Michael I Rangabe.
[^379]: Synaxarion of the Sunday of Orthodoxy (Part 2 — The Miracles of 843 A.D.): Physical leaf p166 (Printed fol. 162). Xanthopoulos chronicles the Second Iconoclasm under Leo V the Armenian (813–820 A.D.), Michael II the Amorian (820–829 A.D.), and Theophilus (829–842 A.D.). Upon Theophilus's agonized deathbed illness and repentance after kissing an enkolpion, Empress Theodora convened the Orthodox hierarchy. Patriarch John the Grammarian (Jannes) was deposed, and St. Methodius I of Constantinople installed as Patriarch. The marginal gloss *панкарпенникъ* (Greek: παγκάρπιον or παγκαρпоθήκη) designates the wicker grain-basket or tomb-like confinement on the island of Antigone in which St. Methodius was imprisoned for seven years prior to his patriarchate.
[^380]: Synaxarion of the Sunday of Orthodoxy (Part 3 — The Divine Absolution and the Triumph): Physical leaf p166 (Printed fol. 162). Xanthopoulos narrates the prophetic mission of the ascetics of Mount Olympus (St. Joannicius the Great, Arsacius, and Isaiah of Nicomedia), who directed Theodora and Methodius to restore the icons. The empress required an all-night intercession throughout the first week of Lent for the soul of her deceased husband Theophilus. In parallel visions, Theodora saw Theophilus pardoned before Christ at the Brazen Gate, while Methodius saw an angel announce the emperor's pardon; when Methodius opened the scroll placed beneath the Holy Table upon which the names of heretical emperors were inscribed, Theophilus's name was miraculously blotted out. The narrative concludes with the historical first Triumph of Orthodoxy procession on March 11, 843 A.D. (the First Sunday of Lent) to the Hippodrome, establishing the annual feast.
[^381]: Odes 7, 8, and 9 of the Matins Canon: Physical leaf p167 (Printed fol. 163). Ode 7 invokes the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace as types of unwavering confession against royal idolatry. Ode 8 articulates the ancestral canonical tradition: "preserving the ancestral laws of the Church, we depict icons... clearly referring the honor and veneration of the icon unto the Prototype." Ode 9 praises the restoration of the Church's adorned beauty and concludes with prayers for the Christian sovereigns and the annulment of Eve's condemnation through the Theotokos.
[^382]: Praises (Ainoi) of Matins: Physical leaf p168 (Printed fol. 164). Four Resurrection stichera and one Anatolian sticheron are followed by three prosomoia of the Triodion in Tone 4, to the melody *Thou hast given a sign* (*Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́меніе*). The second sticheron directly refutes docetic and Manichaean Christology ("the God-fighting children of Manes", *бг҃обо́рніи дѣ́ти Ма́нентовы*), confessing that the Son of God became manifest "not in mere appearance... but in truth and by the nature of flesh," which alone validates the physical depiction of Christ's humanity.
[^383]: Idiomelon of the Day at the Praises: Physical leaf p168 (Printed fol. 164). Composed in Tone 6 (*Мѡѷсе́й во вре́мѧ воздержа́нія...*), this hymn weaves together the great Old Testament types of ascetic fasting: Moses receiving the Law on Mount Sinai (Ex. 34:28), Elijah shutting the heavens and ascending to God (1 Kings 17–19), and the Three Children conquering the fiery Babylonian furnace (Dan. 1–3), concluding with an invocation of the Trisagion hymn as the gateway to the Resurrection.
[^384]: Divine Liturgy of St. Basil the Great and Refectory Rule: Physical leaf p169 (Printed fol. 165). For the First Sunday of Lent, the Liturgy appoints the Beatitudes on 6, Ode 6 of the Triodion Canon on 4; Prokeimenon in Tone 4 (*Song of the Fathers*, Dan. 3:26–27); Epistle to the Hebrews 11:24–26, 32–12:2 (section 324 from the half, recounting the triumphs of faith among the Old Testament prophets and martyrs); Alleluia in Tone 4 (Ps. 98:6); Gospel from John 1:43–51 (section 5, the calling of Philip and Nathanael); and Communion Hymns (Ps. 148:1 and Ps. 32:1). The Trapeza rubric permits cooked food with oil (*варе́ніе со є҆ле́емъ*) and wine in moderation (two cups at dinner and two at supper), while fish remains strictly prohibited.
[^385]: First Sunday of the Great Fast at Vespers: Physical leaf p169 (Printed fol. 165). Display headpiece featuring an ornamental woodcut band. Vespers opens the second week of Lent (*Постѡ́въ ны́нѣ двоседми́чїе свѣ́тлѡ начне́мъ*), setting 10 verses at "Lord, I have cried": 4 penitential stichera of the Octoechos, 2 prosomoia by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (Tone 4, Model: *Thou hast given a sign*), 1 by St. Theodore the Studite (Tone 6, Model: *In archangelic manner* / *А҆рха́гг҃льски*), and 3 from the Menaion. Following the Entrance with the censer, the Great Prokeimenon in Tone 8 is chanted: *Thou hast given an inheritance unto them that fear Thee, O Lord* (*Да́лъ є҆сѝ достоя́ніе боя́щымся тебѐ Гд҃и*, Ps. 60:6). The rubric notes that this Great Prokeimenon alternates with *Turn not away Thy face* on every Sunday evening throughout Great Lent until the Fifth Sunday.
[^386]: Aposticha of Sunday Evening Vespers: Physical leaf p170 (Printed fol. 166). The prayer *Vouchsafe, O Lord* introduces the resumption of the Lenten weekday prostrations and bows (*покло́ны*). The Aposticha idiomelon in Tone 8 (*Прїиди́те, ѡ҆чи́стимъ себѐ...*) sets forth Christ's teaching on secretive, vainglory-free almsgiving from Matthew 6:1–4 ("lest the left hand know what the right hand does"), concluding with the petition to the Father to forgive our trespasses.
[^387]: Compline on the First Sunday of the Great Fast: Physical leaf p170 (Printed fol. 166). Display headpiece featuring an ornamental woodcut band. At Compline, a dedicated Canon to the Holy Prophets is sung in Tone 5, authored by St. Theophanes Graptos, bearing the acrostic: *Ли́къ да пле́щетъ проро́кѡвъ благото́чнѡ* (Greek: *Χορὸς δὲ κρουέτω προφητῶν εὐρύθμως* — "Let the choir of the Prophets clap tunefully"). This canon extends the prophetic commemoration from the Sunday of Orthodoxy into the opening hours of the second week of the Great Fast.
[^388]: Compline on the First Sunday of the Great Fast (Sunday of Orthodoxy), Ode 1 of the Canon to the Holy Prophets conclusion: Physical leaf p171 (Printed fol. 167). The opening strophe of leaf p171 completes the first ode of St. Theophanes's Compline canon with a Theotokion celebrating the prophets as "God-proclaiming trumpets" (*Трꙋбы̑ бг҃огла́сныѧ*) who foreannounced the Virgin's virginal childbearing of the Incarnate Word in the fullness of time.
[^389]: Ode 3 of the Compline Canon to the Holy Prophets: Physical leaf p171 (Printed fol. 167). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Водрꙋзи́вый на ничесо́мже* (Greek: *Ὁ πήξας ἐπ' οὐδενός* — "Who didst establish upon nothing the earth by Thy command"). The hymnody portrays the prophets as spiritual lyres (*цѣвни̑цы дх҃ѡ́вныѧ*) struck by the plectrum of the Holy Spirit, and as mystical chalices (*мы́сленныѧ ча̑шы*) pouring forth the knowledge of the divine dispensation unto all the ends of the earth.
[^390]: Ode 4 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p171 (Printed fol. 167). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Бж҃е́ственное твоѐ разꙋмѣ́въ истоща́нїе* (Greek: *Τὴν θείαν σου ἐννοήσας κένωσιν* — "Perceiving Thy divine kenosis, O Habakkuk"). The troparia celebrate the prophets as unerring torches of truth who foresaw the calling of the Gentiles (*ꙗ҆зы́кѡвъ приведенїе*), the rejection of ancient Israel, and the manifestation of God from the Virgin, depicted as the overshadowing Mountain from Habakkuk 3:3.
[^391]: Ode 5 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p171 (Printed fol. 167). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Ѡ҆дѣѧ́йся свѣ́томъ ꙗ҆́кѡ ри́зою* (Greek: *Ὁ ἀναβαλλόμενος φῶς ὡς ἱμάτιον* — "Thou that clothest Thyself with light as with a garment"). The ode invokes the morning vigil of Isaiah (Isa. 26:9), who beheld the Lord sitting upon the high and lifted up throne surrounded by Seraphim (Isa. 6:1–3), prefiguring the incarnation of the merciful God.
[^392]: Ode 6 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p172 (Printed fol. 168). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Неи́стовствꙋющесѧ бꙋ́рею* (Greek: *Μαινομένην κλύδωνι* — "Raging with the tempest and soul-destroying surge, O Christ"). The troparia celebrate the Old Testament prophets who by divine power restrained the tyranny of kings, stopped the mouths of lions (Daniel), and were delivered unhurt from the belly of the sea-monster (Jonah).
[^393]: Sessional Hymn at Compline: Physical leaf p172 (Printed fol. 168). Tone 2 Automelon (*Ли́къ пррⷪ́ческїй съ Мѡѷсе́омъ и҆ А҆арѡ́номъ* / *Ὁ τῶν προφητῶν χορός*). The hymn unites Moses and Aaron with the prophetic company, rejoicing that the prophetic shadows have reached their consummation in the radiance of the Life-Giving Cross of Christ.
[^394]: Ode 7 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p172 (Printed fol. 168). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Превозноси́мый ѻ҆тце́въ Гдⷭ҇ь* (Greek: *Ὁ ὑπερυψούμενος τῶν πατέρων Κύριος* — "The supremely exalted Lord of our fathers quenched the flame"). The troparion recalls Daniel in the lions' den, while the Theotokion extols the Mother of God as having surpassed the laws of nature by giving birth unto the Lawgiver Himself.
[^395]: Ode 8 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p172 (Printed fol. 168). Heirmos in Tone 5: *Тебѣ̀ всемꙋдѣ́телю* (Greek: *Σοὶ τῷ παντουργῷ* — "Unto Thee, the Creator of all, the children in the furnace sang"). The hymnody depicts the prophets rejoicing in the bliss of Paradise (*внꙋ́трь сла́дости ра́йскїѧ*), serving as fragrant spiritual censers that instruct all nations to praise Christ unto the ages.
[^396]: Ode 9 of the Compline Canon: Physical leaf p172 (Printed fol. 168). Heirmos in Tone 5: *И҆са́їе ликꙋ́й* (Greek: *Ἡσαΐα χόρευε* — "Rejoice, O Isaiah, the Virgin has conceived"). The opening troparion exhorts the faithful to form a single chorus of praise for the prophetic lineage of Jacob, who performed supernatural wonders and foretold the Advent of Christ.
[^397]: Stichera to the Holy Prophets at Compline: Physical leaf p173 (Printed fol. 169). Chanted at the end of Compline in Tone 1, to the automelon *Прехва́льнїи мꙋ́ченицы* (*Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες* — "O all-praised martyrs, whom the earth did not conceal"). The three stanzas draw upon Hebrews 11:37–38, commemorating how the prophets were sawn asunder (*претре́ни*, alluding to the martyrdom of Isaiah), stoned (Jeremiah), and slain with the sword, wandering in deserts and caves. The leaf concludes with an ornamental woodcut tailpiece vignette depicting King David kneeling with his harp.
[^398]: Monday of the Second Week of the Great Fast at Matins (*Въ понедѣ́льникъ в҃-ѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀ на ꙋ҆́трени*): Physical leaf p174 (Printed fol. 170). Display headpiece featuring an ornamental woodcut band depicting Christ the Pantocrator in an architectural frame flanked by foliate vine tendrils. Matins resumes the full weekday Lenten cursus: the Psalter kathismata are read with prostrations, followed by the compunctionate sessional hymns (*сѣда́льны ꙋ҆мили́тельныѧ*) of the Octoechos in the Tone of the week.
[^399]: Sessional Hymns after the Second and Third Kathismata: Physical leaf p174 (Printed fol. 170). After the second kathisma, the Sessional Hymn in Tone 4 (*Престꙋпле́нїѧ плодо́мъ ꙋ҆мерщвле́на дꙋше́ю...*, Model: *Joseph marveled* / *Оу҆диви́сѧ І҆ѡ́сифъ*) laments the spiritual death caused by the fruit of transgression in Eden, praying for vivification through fasting and repentance. After the third kathisma, the Sessional Hymn in Tone 6 (*Гдⷭ҇и напра́ви ны̀ по́стъ повторя́ющыѧ...*, Model: *Lord, have mercy on us*) asks divine guidance for the faithful who are renewing their fast in the second week, concluding with the Annunciation-themed Theotokion of Gabriel's greeting.
[^400]: Canons at Matins of the Second Monday: Physical leaf p175 (Printed fol. 171). The rubrics appoint the canon from the Menaion for the saint of the day, followed by the three-canto Triodion (*трїпѣ́снецъ*) comprising Odes 1, 8, and 9. The first canon is authored by St. Joseph the Hymnographer in Tone 4, and the second by St. Theodore the Studite in Tone 2.
[^401]: Ode 1 of the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph: Physical leaf p175 (Printed fol. 171). Tone 4, Heirmos: *Трїста́ты крѣ́пкїѧ* (Greek: *Τριστάτας κραταιούς* — "The mighty captains of Pharaoh"). St. Joseph defines the true, holistic nature of Christian fasting: not mere abstinence from foods, but "departure from sin, estrangement from passions, love toward God, diligence in prayer, tears with compunction, and care for the poor."
[^402]: Ode 1 of the Triodion Canon by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p175 (Printed fol. 171). Tone 2, Heirmos: *Помо́щникъ и҆ покрови́тель* (Greek: *Βοηθὸς καὶ σκεπαστής* — "He was my helper and protector unto salvation", from the Song of Moses, Ex. 15:2). St. Theodore greets the second week of Lent as a spiritual, non-carnal feast (*непра́здничнꙋю ра́дость* / ἀνέορτον χαρὰν), urging the brethren to enter upon it with compunction and fervent sighing before the approaching judgment of Christ.
[^403]: Ode 8 of the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph: Physical leaf p175 (Printed fol. 171). Tone 4, Heirmos: *И҆зба́вителю всѣ́хъ всеси́льне* (Greek: *Λυτρωτὰ τοῦ παντὸς παντοδύναμε* — "O Redeemer of all, Almighty One"). St. Joseph uses the parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30) and Peter sinking beneath the waves (Matt. 14:30) as archetypes of the soul's desperate need for Christ the divine Physician.
[^404]: Ode 8 of the Triodion Canon by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p176 (Printed fol. 172). Tone 2, Heirmos: *Е҆го́же вѡ́инства нбⷭ҇наѧ сла́вѧтъ* (Greek: *Ὃν στρατιαὶ οὐρανῶν δοξάζουσι* — "Whom the heavenly hosts glorify"). St. Theodore warns the soul against the intemperance of Esau, who sold his birthright for a single bowl of pottage (Gen. 25:29–34), and contrasts water-drinking with the drunkenness of Noah (Gen. 9:20–23) and Lot (Gen. 19:30–38). In the second troparion, the printed Slavonic text reads *ѡ҆богатѝ Велїа́ра воздержа́нїемъ* ("enrich Belial through abstinence"), an evident typographic or recensional corruption for *ѡ҆бнища́й* / *ѡ҆бокрадѝ* (Greek: πτώχευσον Βελίαρ — "impoverish Belial").
[^405]: Ode 9 of the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph: Physical leaf p176 (Printed fol. 172). Tone 4, Heirmos: *Е҆́ѵа ꙋ҆́бѡ недꙋ́гомъ преслꙋша́нїѧ* (Greek: *Εὔα μὲν τῷ τῆς παρακοῆς νοσήματι* — "Eve, indeed, through the disease of disobedience brought in the curse"). St. Joseph exhorts the faithful to extinguish the wrath of the coming judgment through almsgiving, tears of repentance, and the renewal of their baptismal robe.
[^406]: Ode 9 of the Triodion Canon by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p176 (Printed fol. 172). Tone 2, Heirmos: *Безсѣ́меннагѡ Зача́тїѧ Ржⷭ҇тво̀ неска́занное* (Greek: *Ἀσπόρου συλλήψεως ὁ τόκος ἀνερμήνευτος* — "The ineffable Childbirth of a seedless conception"). St. Theodore compares the ascetic fasters to soldiers of Christ (*ꙗ҆́кѡ во́ини Хрⷭ҇то́вы*) contending in the spiritual arena to receive incorruptible crowns from Christ at His glorious second coming.
[^407]: Aposticha at Matins of the Second Monday: Physical leaf p177 (Printed fol. 173). Idiomelon sticheron in Tone 5 (*Ди́вное ѻ҆рꙋ́жїе моли́тва и҆ по́стъ...*). The hymn celebrates prayer and fasting as an invincible spiritual weapon (*ди́вное ѻ҆рꙋ́жїе*), citing the biblical exemplars Moses, who received the Law on Sinai after fasting forty days (Ex. 34:28), and Elijah, who offered acceptable sacrifice on Mount Carmel (1 Kings 18:36–38).
[^408]: Sixth Hour Troparion of the Prophecy: Physical leaf p177 (Printed fol. 173). Tone 5 (*Созда́вый наеди́нѣ сердца̀ на́ша...*), based on Psalm 32:15 (LXX: *ὁ πλάσας κατὰ μόνας τὰς καρδίας αὐτῶν* — "He that fashioned their hearts individually"). The troparion introduces the Sixth Hour Scripture reading, imploring the sinless Creator to remember our mortal dust and spare us from the underworld.
[^409]: Sixth Hour First Prokeimenon: Physical leaf p178 (Printed fol. 174). Tone 6, Psalm 21:23 (LXX: *Боя́щїисѧ Гдⷭ҇а восхвали́те є҆го̀* / *Оἱ φοβούμενοι τὸν Κύριον αἰνέσατε αὐτόν*). The verse is taken from the opening of the great Passion psalm: *O God, my God, attend unto me; why hast Thou forsaken me?* (Ps. 21:1, LXX).
[^410]: Sixth Hour Old Testament Reading from Isaiah: Physical leaf p178 (Printed fol. 174). Isaiah 4:2–5:7 (section 4:2–6 and 5:1–7). This classic Lenten reading combines the Messianic prophecy of the Branch of the Lord who will cleanse the daughters of Zion with the Spirit of judgment and burning, followed by the famous "Song of the Vineyard" (Isa. 5:1–7). God planted a fertile vineyard with choice vines and a watchtower, but it brought forth wild grapes and thorns; therefore the hedge will be removed and the clouds commanded not to rain upon it, prefiguring the judgment upon unrepentant Israel.
[^411]: Sixth Hour Second Prokeimenon: Physical leaf p178 (Printed fol. 174). Tone 6, Psalm 22:4 (LXX: *Же́злъ тво́й и҆ па́лица твоѧ̀, та́ мѧ ꙋ҆тѣ́шиста* / *Ἡ ῥάβδος σου καὶ ἡ βακτηρία σου, αὗταί με παρεκάλεσαν*). The verse is Psalm 22:1 (LXX: *The Lord is my shepherd, and I shall want nothing*).
[^412]: Monday of the Second Week of the Great Fast at Vespers (*Въ понедѣ́льникъ в҃-ѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гѡ вели́кагѡ поста̀ ве́чера*): Physical leaf p178 (Printed fol. 174). Preceded by an ornamental divider band. Vespers appoints the 18th Kathisma of the Psalter (*Ко Гдⷭ҇ꙋ* / *Songs of Ascent*, Psalms 119–133 LXX), followed at "Lord, I have cried" by 6 stichera: 3 from the Triodion (by St. Joseph and St. Theodore) and 3 from the Menaion.
[^413]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: Physical leaf p178 (Printed fol. 174). Tone 8, Podoben: *What shall we call you* (*Что́ вы́ нарече́мъ* / *Τί ὑμᾶς καλέσωμεν*). St. Joseph calls upon the careless and indolent soul to awaken to the reality of the approaching Judgment, urging it to wash away the stains of sin through tears of repentance and fasting before the doors of mercy close.
[^414]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p179 (Printed fol. 175). Tone 8, Podoben: *In Eden of old* (*Во Е҆де́мѣ ра́й* / *Ἐν Ἐδὲμ παράδεισος*). St. Theodore thanks God for granting the faithful to complete the cycle of the first week and cross the threshold into the second week's ascetic arena, praying that all may manfully run the course and attain the glorious Day of the Lord's Resurrection.
[^415]: Vespers First Prokeimenon: Physical leaf p179 (Printed fol. 175). Tone 6, Psalm 23:8 (LXX: *Гдⷭ҇ь крѣ́покъ и҆ си́ленъ, Гдⷭ҇ь си́ленъ въ бра́ни* / *Κύριος κραταιὸς καὶ δυνατός, Κύριος δυνατὸς ἐν πολέμῳ*). The verse is Psalm 23:1 (LXX: *The earth is the Lord's, and the fullness thereof*).
[^416]: Vespers First Old Testament Reading from Genesis: Physical leaf p179 (Printed fol. 175). Genesis 3:21–4:7. The narrative recounts God clothing Adam and Eve with garments of skins (*ри̑зы кѡ́жаны*), their expulsion from Paradise, and the placement of the Cherubim with the turning flaming sword to guard the tree of life. It then describes the birth of Cain and Abel, their sacrifices, God's acceptance of Abel's firstlings and rejection of Cain's fruit of the ground, and God's warning to Cain concerning the lurking snare of sin.
[^417]: Vespers Second Prokeimenon: Physical leaf p179 (Printed fol. 175). Tone 6, Psalm 24:16 (LXX: *При́зри на мѧ̀, и҆ поми́лꙋй мѧ̀* / *Ἐπίβλεψον ἐπ' ἐμὲ καὶ ἐλέησόν με*). The verse is Psalm 24:1 (LXX: *Unto Thee, O Lord, have I lifted up my soul*).
[^418]: Vespers Second Old Testament Reading from Proverbs: Physical leaf p179 (Printed fol. 175). Proverbs 3:34–4:22 (opening section: 3:34–35 and 4:1–5). King Solomon teaches the path of divine wisdom, warning against the proud and calling the youth to hold fast to parental instruction as the foundation of life and glory.
[^419]: Aposticha at Monday Evening Vespers: Physical leaf p180 (Printed fol. 176). Idiomelon sticheron of the day in Tone 3 (*Дꙋхо́внымъ посто́мъ пости́мсѧ...*), sung twice. The hymn sets forth the gospel law of mutual forgiveness (Matt. 6:14–15) as the indispensable prerequisite for authentic fasting: by forgiving our brethren their debts, our prayer ascends before God as fragrant evening incense (Ps. 140:2).
[^420]: Typikon Rubric on the Displacement of Menaion Canons at Compline: Physical leaf p180 (Printed fol. 176). The rubric (*Зрѝ: Вѣ́домо же да є҆́сть...*) codifies the ancient Studite-Sabbaitic rule for resolving calendar collisions during Great Lent. Beginning on the second Monday of the Great Fast, the canons of saints commemorated in the Menaion during the liturgical window between Lazarus Saturday and Thomas Sunday (when the festal services of Holy Week and Bright Week completely displace the Menaion) are transferred and chanted in advance at weekday Compline throughout Lent. The order prescribes first the Theotokion canon, followed by the saint's canon from the Menaion, with the saint's Kontakion and Sessional Hymn after Ode 3, the Theotokion Sessional Hymn after Ode 6, and the Menaion stichera at the conclusion of the canon.
[^421]: Tuesday of the Second Week of the Great Fast at Matins (*Во вто́рникъ в҃-ѧ седми́цы ст҃а́гw вели́кагw поста̀ на оу́трени*): Physical leaf p181 (Printed fol. 177). Display headpiece featuring an ornamental woodcut band depicting an standing figure (prophet or saint) framed in an architectural arch flanked by grapevines and floral tendrils. The rubrics appoint the reading of the Psalter kathismata followed by the compunctionate sessional hymns of the Octoechos in the Tone of the week (Tone 8) with their Theotokia.
[^422]: Sessional Hymns after the Second Psalter Reading: Physical leaf p181 (Printed fol. 177). Tone 8, Podoben: "Thou hast risen" (*Воскре́слъ є҆сѝ* / *Ἀνέστης ἐκ νεκρῶν*). The troparion calls the faithful to restrain bodily passions through the blameless bridle of fasting in order to elevate the noetic faculties toward divine contemplation with unshakeable faith. The accompanying Theotokion laments the depth of despondency caused by sinful deeds, invoking the Mother of God as the sole propitiation and saving purification of sinners.
[^423]: Sessional Hymn after the Third Psalter Reading: Physical leaf p181 (Printed fol. 177). Tone 8, Podoben: "The shepherd's pipe" (*Па́стырскаѧ свирѣ́ль* / *Ὁ ποιμενικὸς αὐλός*). The hymn describes Great Lent as a mystical banquet table spread by God, inviting the faithful to partake richly of the vivifying gifts of the Holy Spirit as food and divine tears as spiritual drink.
[^424]: Ode 2 of the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: Physical leaf p181 (Printed fol. 177). Tone 8, Heirmos: *Ви́дите ви́дите, ꙗ҆́кw а҆́зъ є҆́смь бг҃ъ ва́шъ* (Greek: *Ἴδετε ἴδετε, ὅτι ἐγώ εἰμι ὁ Θεὸς ὑμῶν* — "See, see that I am your God, born of the Father before the ages, and in these latter times conceived of the Virgin without seed of man, Who destroyed the sin of our forefather Adam, as the Lover of mankind", based on Deut. 32:39). St. Joseph urges the soul to hasten in repentance because the Judge stands at the door and the day of reckoning is at hand.
[^425]: Ode 2 of the Second Triodion Canon by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p182 (Printed fol. 178). Tone 8, Heirmos: *Ви́дите ви́дите* (*Ἴδετε ἴδετε*). St. Theodore reminds the ascetic of the imminent tribunal of Christ and contrasts the salvation of the repentant Ninevites through fasting with the fiery destruction of the self-indulgent Sodomites. The canto concludes with St. Theodore's own full text of the biblical heirmos (Deut. 32:39), which explicitly characterizes Lent as a spiritual tithing (*ѡ҆десѧтствова́нїе*) of the whole year given unto God's people for the eradication of the passions.
[^426]: Ode 8 of the Triodion Canon by St. Joseph: Physical leaf p182 (Printed fol. 178). Tone 8, Heirmos: *Бг҃оглаго́ливіи ѻ҆́троцы* (Greek: *Οἱ θεορρήμονες Παῖδες* — "The God-proclaiming children in the furnace trampled down the flame"). The troparia set forth the Publican and the Harlot as models of compunction, summoning all nine orders of angels, apostles, and martyrs to intercede for the forgiveness of sins.
[^427]: Ode 8 of the Triodion Canon by St. Theodore the Studite: Physical leaf p183 (Printed fol. 179). Tone 8, Heirmos: *На горѣ̀ ст҃ѣ́й просла́вльшасѧ* (Greek: *Τὸν ἐν ὄρει ἁγίῳ δοξασθέντα* — "Him Who was glorified on the holy mountain, and in the bush through fire revealed the mystery of the Ever-Virgin unto Moses").
